Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

That’s kind of why I came in — I keep leaving my seat in those longer sales meetings, even if it’s just to refill water or stand near the printer for a minute.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like getting up and moving around during those meetings is something you can't control?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yeah. I mean, I know I’m supposed to stay seated, but in those long sales strategy meetings I still end up getting up—refilling water or pacing by the printer. It doesn’t feel totally in my control.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind and are hard to get rid of?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I get stuck on work things sometimes, but not like thoughts repeating over and over that I can’t get rid of.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these things, like getting up or refilling your water, in response to some kind of internal rule or urge?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like a rule, exactly. It’s more just… I’m in those long sales strategy meetings, and I know I should stay seated, but I end up getting up anyway—like to refill my water or just stand by the printer for a minute.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up and moving around during those meetings because you're trying to make something happen or prevent something from happening?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’m trying to make anything happen or prevent something. It’s more… the work thing. The long sales strategy meetings are what made me notice it—I keep getting up even when I know I’m supposed to stay seated, like to refill water or just walk over by the printer.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel restless when you're doing quiet activities, like reading or watching a movie?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It doesn’t feel totally in my control.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind and are hard to get rid of?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I get stuck on work things sometimes, but not like thoughts repeating over and over that I can’t get rid of.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these things, like getting up or refilling your water, in response to some kind of internal rule or urge?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like a rule, exactly. It’s more just… I’m in those long sales strategy meetings, and I know I should stay seated, but I end up getting up anyway—like to refill my water or just stand by the printer for a minute.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up and moving around during those meetings because you're trying to make something happen or prevent something from happening?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’m trying to make anything happen or prevent something. It’s more that in long sales strategy meetings, I just end up getting up even though I know I’m supposed to stay seated—like refilling water or pacing by the printer.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like getting up and moving around is something you do to relieve a feeling of restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not like a big panic feeling or anything, but in those long sales strategy meetings I just get uncomfortable sitting there, and I end up getting up—refilling water, pacing by the printer for a minute—even though I know I’m supposed to stay seated.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel restless or feel the need to get up and move around in situations other than those long sales strategy meetings?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s those long strategy meetings. The thing that stands out is the long strategy meetings at work—I keep getting up when I know I’m supposed to stay seated, even if it’s just water or walking over by the printer.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel restless or fidgety when you're at home or doing things you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really at home, no. It’s more… the work thing. I haven’t noticed that with reading or watching movies.\n\nDoctor: Does this feeling of restlessness and need to get up happen in any other parts of your life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. It’s mainly those long sales strategy meetings—like I know I’m supposed to stay seated, but I’ll still get up to refill water or just walk near the printer.\n\nDoctor: Does this restlessness happen when you're in meetings or situations that are not related to work?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed. It’s mostly the work meetings—the long strategy ones.
